Kondo Lattice Model of Magic-Angle Twisted-Bilayer Graphene: Hund’s Rule, Local-Moment Fluctuations, and Low-Energy Effective Theory
Haoyu Hu, B. Andrei Bernevig, Alexei M. Tsvelik

Abstract
We apply a generalized Schrieffer–Wolff transformation to the extended Anderson-like topological heavy fermion (THF) model for the magic-angle ( θ = 1.05 ∘ ) (\theta=1.05^{\circ}) twisted bilayer graphene (MATBLG) (Phys. Rev. Lett. 129 , 047601 (2022)), to obtain its Kondo Lattice limit. In this limit localized f f -electrons on a triangular lattice interact with topological conduction c c -electrons. By solving the exact limit of the THF model, we show that the integer fillings ν = 0 , ± 1 , ± 2 \nu=0,\pm 1,\pm 2 are controlled by the heavy f f -electrons, while ν = ± 3 \nu=\pm 3 is at the border of a phase transition between two f f -electron fillings. For ν = 0 , ± 1 , ± 2 \nu=0,\pm 1,\pm 2 , we then calculate the RKKY interactions between the f f -moments in the full model and analytically prove the SU(4) Hund’s rule for the ground state which maintains that two f f -electrons fill the same valley-spin flavor. Our (ferromagnetic interactions in the) spin model dramatically differ from the usual Heisenberg antiferromagnetic interactions expected at strong coupling. We show the ground state in some limits can be found exactly by employing a positive semidefinite ”bond-operators” method. We then compute the excitation spectrum of the f f -moments in the ordered ground state, prove the stability of the ground state favored by RKKY interactions, and discuss the properties of the Goldstone modes, the (reason for the accidental) degeneracy of (some of) the excitation modes, and the physics of their phase stiffness. We develop a low-energy effective theory for the f f -moments and obtain analytic expressions for the dispersion of the collective modes. We discuss the relevance of our results to the spin-entropy experiments in twisted bilayer graphene.

C_{2z}T
or
P
) becomes non-local.
To address this problem
the authors of Ref.
Song and Bernevig 2022
have introduced an exact mapping of MATBG to a THF model. THF is a version of the extended Anderson lattice model describing localized
f
-electrons interacting with topological conduction
c
-electrons. The
f
-electrons have zero kinetic energy and strong Hubbard interactions; they admit a description in terms of localized Wannier orbitals centered at the AA-stacking region.
The topological flat bands can be recovered from the hybridization between the
f
- and the
c
-electrons
Song and Bernevig 2022
.

In this letter, we map the THF model to a Kondo lattice model using a generalized Schrieffer-Wolff (SW) transformation which takes into account the density-density interaction term between
f
- and
c
-electrons. In this limit, the dynamics of the localized orbitals becomes the one of the
f
-moments. By solving exactly a particular limit of the THF model, we show that the integer fillings
\nu=0,\pm 1,\pm 2
are controlled by the
f
-electrons, while the situation is drastically different for
\nu=\pm 3
which sits at the phase transition between two
f
-electrons fillings.
In the Kondo lattice model of MATBLG, the local moments formed by the fully-localized
f
-electrons interact with topological conduction electron bands via Kondo superexchange and direct ferromagnetic exchange interaction. These two types of exchange interactions induce an RKKY interaction. At
\nu=0,-1,-2
, the RKKY interaction dominates the physics and stabilizes the ferromagnetic ground states that obey the Hund’s rule.
This result provides an analytic derivation of the Hund’s rule found numerically in the Hartree-Fock calculations of THF model
Song and Bernevig 2022
.
We then proceed to investigate fluctuations of the
f
-moments
in the symmetry broken ground states
by developing the low-energy effective theory and calculating the excitation spectrum.

Schrieffer–Wolff transformation and Kondo lattice model—

The single-particle Hamiltonian of the THF model contains the kinetic term
{\hat{H}}_{c}
describing the topological conduction
c
-electron bands (SM
SM
, Sec. I), and the hybridization between the
f
- and the
c
-electrons
{\hat{H}}_{fc}

Song and Bernevig 2022
;
SM
:

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{fc}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},\mathbf{R}\\
{i,\xi,\xi^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\bigg(\frac{e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}-\frac{|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}}\tilde{H}_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}^{(fc)}(\mathbf{k})}{\sqrt{N_{M}}}\psi_{\mathbf{R},i}^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{k},i}^{c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}+\text{h.c.}\bigg)

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(fc)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{bmatrix}\gamma&v_{\star}^{\prime}(k_{x}-ik_{y})\\
v_{\star}^{\prime}(k_{x}+ik_{y})&\gamma\end{bmatrix}\,.

(1)

where
\psi_{\mathbf{k},i}^{c^{\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}
creates
\Gamma_{3}

\mathbf{k}
, valley-spin flavor

(with

(+\uparrow,-\uparrow,+\downarrow,-\downarrow)

Song and Bernevig 2022

\psi_{\mathbf{R},i}^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}

\mathbf{R}
with valley-spin flavor

\xi=(-1)^{a+1}\eta

Song and Bernevig 2022

\Lambda_{c}

Song and Bernevig 2022

In the hybridization matrix
\tilde{H}^{(fc)}(\mathbf{k})

\gamma

) in the expansion in powers of
\mathbf{k}
.
The flat band limit is realized by setting

is taken as a parameter of
{\hat{H}}_{c}

The interaction Hamiltonian of the THF model is

{\hat{H}}_{I}={\hat{H}}_{U}+{\hat{H}}_{J}+{\hat{H}}_{V}+{\hat{H}}_{W}
,
where
{\hat{H}}_{U},{\hat{H}}_{J}
describe respectively the on-site Hubbard interaction of the
f
-electrons
(

- and the
c

(

{\hat{H}}_{V},{\hat{H}}_{W}
describe respectively the repulsion between the

-electrons
(
\sim 48

and the repulsion between the
f
- and the
c
-electrons
(
W=47
meV)
Song and Bernevig 2022
;
Călugăru et al.
.
The full Hamiltonian is
{\hat{H}}_{c}+{\hat{H}}_{fc}+{\hat{H}}_{I}
. The model possesses the
U(4)\times U(4)
symmetry in the chiral-flat limit (
M=0
,
v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
), a flat

U(4)
symmetry in the nonchiral-flat limit (
M=0,v_{\star}^{\prime}\neq 0
), a chiral
U(4)
symmetry in the chiral-nonflat limit
(M\neq 0,v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
) and a
U(2)\times U(2)
symmetry in the nonchiral-nonflat limit
(M\neq 0,v_{\star}^{\prime}\neq 0
)
Song and Bernevig 2022
;
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Lian et al. 2021
;
Bultinck et al. 2020b
;
Vafek and Kang 2020
;
Vafek and Kang 2021
;
SM
.

At sufficiently strong on-site Coulomb interaction
U
, the
f
-electrons are fully localized and give rise to local
f
-moments which are defined
as

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})=\sum_{i,j}\frac{1}{2}T_{ij}^{\mu\nu}\psi_{\mathbf{R},i}^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{R},j}^{f,\xi^{\prime}}\,.

(2)

\{T^{\mu\nu}_{ij}\}
with
\mu,\nu\in\{0,x,y,z\}
are given in SM
SM
, Sec. I.
Eq.
2
are the generators of the
U(8)

8=2\text{(orbital)}\times 2\text{(valley)}\times 2\text{(spin)}
) where the
U(1)

We first analyze the zero-hybridization limit of the model (
\gamma=0,v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
).
v_{\star}^{\prime}
is small
Song and Bernevig 2022
while
\gamma
changes rapidly and goes through zero at the value of
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.9
close to the actual MATBLG value
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8

Călugăru et al.
. Hence this limit can be thought as a meaningful approximation close to the MATBLG.
The zero-hybridization model is exactly solvable at zero Coulomb repulsion between
c
-electrons.
Here, we treat
{\hat{H}}_{V}
in the mean-field approximation (
{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
) and drop the
{\hat{H}}_{J}
which is relatively weak.
We then solve the model under the assumption that each site is filled with
\nu_{f}+4

f
-electrons with an integer
\nu_{f}

(SM.
SM
, Sec. II). We use
\nu_{c}
to denote the filling of the
c
-electrons and use
\nu=\nu_{f}+\nu_{c}
to denote the total fillings.
In Fig.
1
(a), we plot
\nu_{f}
and
\nu_{c}
of the ground state as a function of
\nu
. At
\nu=0,-1,-2,-3
, the ground state has
\nu_{f}=\nu
and
\nu_{c}=0
.

\nu=-3
is close to the transition point between
\nu_{f}=-3
state and
\nu_{f}=-2

Thus, at
\nu=-3

Xie et al. 2022

This is consistent with the special place that
\nu=-3

Xie et al. 2022

At
\nu=0,-1,-2

f
-electrons (according to Fig.
1

SM
, Sec. IV), which leads to
the following Kondo lattice Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{Kondo}={\hat{H}}_{c}+{\hat{H}}_{cc}+{\hat{H}}_{K}+{\hat{H}}_{J}+{\hat{H}}^{MF}_{V}+{\hat{H}}_{W}

(3)

where
{\hat{H}}_{K}

respectively
Schrieffer and Wolff 1966
;
SM

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{K}=\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\mu\nu\xi\xi^{\prime}}\frac{e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{R}}e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{k}|^{2}+|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}|^{2}}{2}\lambda^{2}}}{N_{M}}

\displaystyle\bigg[\frac{\gamma^{2}}{D_{\nu_{c},\nu_{f}}}:{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})::{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}):+

(4)

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{v_{\star}^{\prime}\gamma(k_{x}-i\xi k_{y})}{D_{\nu_{c},\nu_{f}}}:{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})::{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}-\xi)}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}):+\text{h.c.}\bigg)\bigg]

where the colon symbols represent the normal ordering and

The parameter

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D_{\nu_{c}=0,\nu_{f}}}=-\frac{1}{(U-W)\nu_{f}-\frac{U}{2}}+\frac{1}{(U-W)\nu_{f}+\frac{U}{2}}\,.

(5)

The distinct feature of this expression is the presence

The zeroth order Kondo coupling

\gamma^{2}/D_{\nu_{c}=0,\nu_{f}}=42.3\mathrm{meV},49.3\mathrm{meV},98.6\mathrm{meV}

The RKKY interactions and the Hund’s rule—

By integrating out the c-electrons in the Kondo Hamiltonian(Eq.
3
),
one induces an RKKY interaction between the
f
-moments
Ruderman and Kittel 1954
;
Kasuya 1956
;
Yosida 1957
. We restrict ourselves to deriving the RKKY interactions
SM
in the leading order in
{\hat{H}}_{K},{\hat{H}}_{J}
at integer fillings
\nu=0,-1,-2
.
In the chiral-flat limit (
v_{\star}^{\prime}=0,M=0
), the RKKY interactions can be described by the following
U(4)\times U(4)
symmetric Hamiltonian

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{RKKY}^{v_{\star}^{\prime}=0,M=0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\\
\mu\nu,\xi\xi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}[J^{RKKY}_{0}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})+J^{RKKY}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})\delta_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle:\Sigma_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})::\Sigma_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{R}^{\prime}):\,,

(6)

where both
J^{RKKY}_{0}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})(\leq 0)
and
J^{RKKY}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})(\leq 0)
can be analytically obtained and are ferromagnetic (SM
SM
, Sec.V). Using bond operators
A_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}^{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}=\sum_{i}\psi_{\mathbf{R},i}^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},i}^{f,\xi^{\prime}}
, we show that
{\hat{H}}_{RKKY}^{v_{\star}^{\prime}=0,M=0}
is a Positive Semidefinite Hamiltonian
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
, where the exact ground state can be obtained
SM
;
Bernevig et al. 2021a
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
;
Lian et al. 2021
.
The grounds states
are ferromagnetic with the form of (SM
SM
, Sec. VI)

\displaystyle\prod_{\mathbf{R}}\bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{\nu_{f}^{+}+2}\psi_{\mathbf{R},i_{n}}^{f,+,{\dagger}}\prod_{m=\nu_{f}^{+}+3}^{\nu_{f}+4}\psi_{\mathbf{R},i_{m}}^{f,-,{\dagger}}\bigg]|0\rangle\,.

(7)

where
\nu_{f}^{+}
denotes the filling of the
f
-electrons with index
\xi=1
, and
\nu_{f}^{+}=0
at
\nu=0
,
\nu_{f}^{+}=-1,0
at
\nu=-1
, and
\nu_{f}^{+}=-1
at
\nu=-2
.
\{i_{n}\}
are chosen arbitrarily and
|0\rangle
is the vacuum with
\psi_{\mathbf{R},i}^{f,\xi}|0\rangle=0
. We note that our ground states form a subset of the ground states in Ref.
Lian et al. 2021
, due to the additional non-zero kinetic energy generated by
f
-
c
hybridizations in our model.

We then consider the nonchiral-flat limit (
v_{\star}^{\prime}\neq 0
,
M=0
), where the RKKY interactions are flat-
U(4)
symmetric. They lift the ground-state degeneracy in
Eq.
7
. We obtain the ground states by treating
v_{\star}^{\prime}
-induced RKKY interactions as perturbations (SM
SM
, Sec. VI)
, and the true ground state is selected by the following RKKY interactions

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\\
\mu\nu,\xi\end{subarray}}J^{RKKY}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}):{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi)}(\mathbf{R})::{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,-\xi-\xi)}(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{R}^{\prime}):

(8)

where
J_{2}^{RKKY}(\mathbf{R})(\leq 0)
is analytically obtained and ferromagnetic (SM
SM
, Sec. V).
J^{RKKY}_{2}(\mathbf{R}_{2}-\mathbf{R})
tends to align two
f
-moments
:{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,++)}(\mathbf{R}):
and
:{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,--)}(\mathbf{R}_{2}):

and stabilize the ground states obeying the Hund’s rule (see Fig.
1
).
The corresponding ground states are consistent with Ref.
Lian et al. 2021
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
(nonchiral-flat limit).

Moreover, we also derive the RKKY interactions in the zero-hybridization limit (
\gamma=0,v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
) with non-zero
J(\neq 0)
(SM
SM
, Sec. III), where the corresponding ground states are consistent with Ref.
Lian et al. 2021
(chiral-nonflat limit).

Fluctuations of the
f
-moments—

We check the stability of the ferromagnetic ground state derived from RKKY Hamiltonian by studying small fluctuations.
We restrict ourselves to the flat-
U(4)
nonchiral-flat limit
M=0,v_{\star}^{\prime}\neq 0
at
\nu=0,-1,-2
.
To describe the fluctuations we introduce for each site a
8\times 8
traceless Hermitian matrix
u_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R})
, where

i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
are valley-spin flavors
and
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\{+,-\}
. The
f
-moments in Eq.
2
can then be written as (SM
SM
, Sec. VIII).

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})=\sum_{ij}T^{\mu\nu}_{ij}[e^{iu(\mathbf{R})}\Lambda e^{-iu(\mathbf{R})}]_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}

(9)

where
\Lambda
is an
8\times 8
matrix defined as
\Lambda_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}=\langle\psi_{0}|:\psi_{\mathbf{R},i}^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{R},j}^{f,\xi^{\prime}}:|\psi_{0}\rangle/2

and
|\psi_{0}\rangle
is the ground state.
A non-zero
u_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R})
will generate fluctuations by rotating the
f
-moments from their ground-state expectation values.

The ferromagnetic order in the ground state
opens a gap in the single-particle spectrum of the
c
-electrons which allows us to safely integrate them out
SM
and to develop an effective theory for small fluctuations by expanding the action to the second order in
u_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R},\tau)
, where
\tau
is the imaginary time (SM
SM
, Sec. VIII).
The Lagrangian of the effective theory is provided in SM
SM
, Sec. VIII,
where we find the diagonal components
u_{i\xi,i\xi}(\mathbf{R},\tau)
only contribute a total derivative and we will focus on the off-diagonal components:
u_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R},\tau)
with
i\xi\neq j\xi^{\prime}
.

We then introduce two sets
S_{fill}
and
S_{emp}
to
characterize the ground state, where
S_{fill}
and
S_{emp}
denote the sets of
i\xi
indices that are filled with one and zero number of the
f
-electrons at each site, respectively.
A fluctuation generated by
u_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R})
(
i\xi\neq j\xi^{\prime}
) is described by the procedure of moving one
f
-electron from
j\xi^{\prime}
flavor at site
\mathbf{R}
to
i\xi
flavor at the same site (SM
SM
, Sec. VIII).
This procedure can only be valid when
i\xi\in S_{emp},j\xi^{\prime}\in S_{fill}
.
Consequently, only
u_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R},\tau)
with
j\xi^{\prime}\in S_{fill},i\xi\in S_{emp}
and also its complex conjugate
u_{j\xi^{\prime},i\xi}(\mathbf{R},\tau)
that describes the reverse procedure appear in our effective Lagrangian.
We diagonalize the Lagrangian and plot the excitation spectrum in Fig.
2
.

We first analyze the spectrum at
\nu=0,-2
. The Lagrangian density in the long-wavelength limit and in momentum
(\mathbf{k})
and frequency
(\omega)
spaces has the form of
L=L_{Goldstone}+L_{gapped}
:

\displaystyle L_{Goldstone}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\xi\in{S}_{fill}\\
i\xi\in{S}_{emp}\end{subarray}}u_{j,i}^{\dagger}({\bf k},\omega)\Big(\omega-\frac{k^{2}}{2m_{0}}\Big)u_{j,i}({\bf k},\omega),

\displaystyle L_{gapped}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\xi\in{S}_{fill}\\
i\xi\in{S}_{emp}\end{subarray}}U_{j,i}^{\dagger}({\bf k},\omega)[\omega\hat{I}-\hat{H}(\mathbf{k})]U_{j,i}({\bf k},\omega),

\displaystyle H(\mathbf{k})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{k_{+}k_{-}}{2m_{1}}+\Delta_{1}&Vk_{+}&-Vk_{-}\\
Vk_{-}&\frac{k_{+}k_{-}}{2m_{2}}+\Delta_{2}&\frac{k_{-}^{2}}{2m_{3}}\\
-Vk_{+}&\frac{k_{+}^{2}}{2m_{3}}&\frac{k_{+}k_{-}}{2m_{2}}+\Delta_{2}\end{array}\right)

where
u_{j,i}=(u_{(j+,i+)}+u_{(j-,i-)})/\sqrt{2}
, and
U_{j,i}^{T}=((u_{(j+,i+)}-u_{(j-,i-)})/\sqrt{2},u_{(j+,i-)},u_{(j-,i+)})
and
k_{\pm}=k_{x}\pm ik_{y}
,
k=|\mathbf{k}|
.

m_{0},m_{1},m_{2},V,\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},V

are analytically defined constants that depend on the parameters of the original THF Hamiltonian (SM
SM
, Sec. VIII). The Goldstone modes with quadratic dispersion
decouple from the rest (gapped modes). Their stiffness is
1/m_{0}=5.9\mathrm{meV}\cdot a_{M}^{2}
(at
\nu=0
),
13.2\mathrm{meV}\cdot a_{M}^{2}
(at
\nu=-2
), where
a_{M}
is the moiré lattice constant.

The gaps at
\mathbf{k}=0
are
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, with
\Delta_{2}
corresponding to two-fold degenerate modes. This feature is reproduced in Fig.
2
(c). The exceptional case when all three modes are degenerate (
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}
) is depicted in Fig.
2
(a) and the condition for the degeneracy is
\alpha=[-0.37+\sqrt{0.14+0.23(v_{\star}^{\prime})^{2}/(\gamma\lambda)^{2}}]\gamma^{2}/(JD_{\nu_{c}\nu_{f}})=1
. Using the realistic values of parameters, we find
\alpha=1.07\approx 1
.
By directly evaluating the Lagrangian, we also observe ”roton” minima in the gapped modes (most obviously at
\nu=-2
) at
|\mathbf{k}|\sim 0.3|\bm{b}_{M1}|
with
\bm{b}_{M1}
the moiré reciprocal lattice vector.
The roton mode has small anisotropy with the minimum located along the
\Gamma_{M}
-
M_{M}
line.

We next discuss the number of the Goldstone modes. Each combination of
(i,j)
that satisfies
i+,i-\in S_{emp}
,
j+,j-\in S_{fill}
produce a Goldstone mode
SM
. This leads to
four Goldstone modes at
\nu=0
and three Goldstone modes at
\nu=-2

Bernevig et al. 2021c
.
Furthermore, all excitation modes depicted in Fig.
2
are four-fold degenerate at
\nu=0
and three-fold degenerate at
\nu=-2
, due to the remaining
U(2)\times U(2)
symmetries at
\nu=0
and the remaining
U(1)\times U(3)
symmetries at
\nu=-2
.

We now analyze the spectrum at
\nu=-1
. Unlike
\nu=0,-2
where each valley-spin flavor is filled with either two or zero
f
- electrons, at
\nu=-1
, there is one valley-spin flavor (denoted by
i=1
) filled with two
f
-electrons and one valley-spin flavor (denoted by
i=2
) filled with one
f
-electron and two empty valley-spin flavors (denoted by
i=3,4
) as shown in Fig.
1
(c).
This allows us to classify

u_{i\xi,j\xi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R},\tau)
at
\nu=-1
into four sectors: (1) full-empty sector with
i=3,4,j=1
or
i=1,j=3,4
; (2) half-empty sector with
i=2,j=3,4
or
i=3,4,j=2
; (3) full-half sector with
i=1,j=2
or
j=2,i=1
; (4) half-half sector with
i=2,j=2
.
In Fig.
2
, we label the excitation in different sectors with different colors. Due to the remaining
U(1)\times U(1)\times U(2)
symmetry of the
\nu=-1
ground state
, each mode is two-fold degenerate in the full-empty and half-empty sectors and is non-degenerate in the full-half and half-half sectors. We find
2
degenerate Goldstone modes with stiffness
6.7\mathrm{meV}\cdot{a_{M}^{2}}
in the full-empty sector,
2
degenerate Goldstone modes with stiffness
1.3\mathrm{meV}\cdot a_{M}^{2}
in the half-empty sector, and
1
Goldstone mode with stiffness
1.8\mathrm{meV}\cdot{a_{M}^{2}}
in the full-half sector
Bernevig et al. 2021c
.

Several remarks are in order. Firstly, some of the gapped modes at
\nu=0,-1,-2
are relatively flat as shown in Fig.
2
.
Secondly, at
\nu=-1
, one of the flat modes (green curve in Fig.
2
(b)) with eigenfunction
u_{2-,2+}(\mathbf{k})
has a tiny gap
0.12\text{meV}
at
\Gamma_{M}
point and a very narrow bandwidth
1.5
meV.
Thirdly, the dispersion along
M_{M}
to
K_{M}
is also relatively flat which is related to the approximate
C_{\infty}
symmetry of the excitation modes.

Summary and discussions—

We have constructed and studied a Kondo lattice model for MATBLG. Its distinct feature is the Dirac character of the
c
-electron spectrum: at integer fillings, there is no Fermi surface.
Hence the Kondo screening is irrelevant and the low energy physics is dominated by the RKKY interactions, which is also responsible for the Hund’s rule and ferromagnetism of the ground states. We have developed an effective theory describing small fluctuations of the local moments and found their excitation spectrum. We have also discussed the properties of the Goldstone and gapped modes and their degeneracies.
We believe that our work provides insight into the correlated ground states and the local-moment fluctuations of the MATBLG.

We also comment on the connection with previous works
Bernevig et al. 2021c
;
Khalaf et al. 2020
. Some features, including the soft modes and accidental degeneracy of gapped modes at
\Gamma_{M}
, also appear in the projected Coulomb model
Bernevig et al. 2021c
, but the roton modes do not. We note that the projected Coulomb model
Bernevig et al. 2021c
has ignored the effect of remote bands.
Roton modes have also been seen in Ref.
Khalaf et al. 2020
, where the remote bands have been included. However, our model gives a larger bandwidth of the gapped modes than Ref.
Khalaf et al. 2020
. We point out that, we take a different set of parameter values (including dielectric constant, and gate distance).
Besides, in our model, all Goldstone modes have quadratic dispersions, in contrast to Ref.
Khalaf et al. 2020
which found a linear one. The difference in the results has its origin in the different symmetry properties. We consider the flat limit of the model, and the quadratic dispersion comes from the broken flat
U(4)
symmetry. Ref.
Khalaf et al. 2020
takes non-flat bands, and the linear Goldstone modes come from the broken
U(1)
valley symmetry.

We conclude the paper with a discussion of the relevance of our results to the recent entropy experiments
Saito et al. 2021b
;
Rozen et al. 2021
. Experimentally, the entropy in TBG near
\nu=-1
has been found to be of the order of Boltzmann’s constant and is suppressed by the applications of magnetic fields. This large entropy can be explained by the presence of soft mode at
\nu=-1
, which will be suppressed by the magnetic field.
Finally, we point out that the fluctuations of the
f
-moments could potentially generate attractive interactions between
c
-electrons and drive the system to the superconducting phase.
Thus, our work also establishes a platform for understanding the
superconductivity.

Note added—

After finishing this work, we have learned that related, but not identical, results had recently been obtained by the S. Das Sarma’s
Chou and Sarma 2022
, P. Coleman’s
Lau and Coleman
and Z. Song’s groups
Zhou and Song
.

Supplementary Materials

S1
Model and notation

The topological heavy-fermion model introduced in Ref.
Song and Bernevig 2022
takes the following Hamiltonian

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}={\hat{H}}_{c}+{\hat{H}}_{fc}+{\hat{H}}_{U}+{\hat{H}}_{J}+{\hat{H}}_{W}+{\hat{H}}_{V}

(S1)

The single-particle Hamiltonian of conduction
c
-electrons has the form of

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{c}=\sum_{\eta,s,a,a^{\prime},|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}H_{a,a^{\prime}}^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta s}\quad,\quad H^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{bmatrix}0_{2\times 2}&v_{\star}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{0}+ik_{y}\sigma_{z})\\
v_{\star}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{0}-ik_{y}\sigma_{z})&M\sigma_{x}.\end{bmatrix}

(S2)

where
\sigma_{0,x,y,z}
are identity and Pauli matrices.

c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}
represents the annihilation operator of the
a(=1,2,3,4)
-th conduction band basis of the valley
\eta(=\pm)
and spin
s(=\uparrow,\downarrow)
at the moiré momentum
\mathbf{k}
. At
\Gamma_{M}
point (
\mathbf{k}=0
) of the moiré Brillouin zone,
c_{\mathbf{k}1\eta s},c_{\mathbf{k}2\eta s}
form a
\Gamma_{3}
irreducible representation (of
P6^{\prime}2^{\prime}2
group),

c_{\mathbf{k}3\eta s},c_{\mathbf{k}4\eta s}
form a
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
reducible (into
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
- as they are written, the
c_{\mathbf{k}3\eta s},c_{\mathbf{k}4\eta s}
are just the
\sigma_{x}
linear combinations of
\Gamma_{1}\pm\Gamma_{2}
) representation (of
P6^{\prime}2^{\prime}2
group).
\Lambda_{c}
is the momentum cutoff for the
c
-electrons.
N
is the total number of moiré unit cells.
Parameter values are

v_{\star}=-4.303\mathrm{eV}\cdot\mathrm{\mathring{A}}
,
M=3.697
meV.

The hybridization between
f
and
c
electrons has the form of

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{fc}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{M}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}\\
\mathbf{R}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\alpha a\eta s}\bigg(e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}-\frac{|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}}H^{(fc,\eta)}_{\alpha a}(\mathbf{k})f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}+h.c.\bigg)\ ,

(S3)

where
f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}
represents the annihilation operators of the
f
electrons with orbital index
\alpha(=1,2)
, valley index
\eta(=\pm)
and spin
s(=\uparrow,\downarrow)
at the moiré unit cell
\mathbf{R}
.
N_{M}
is the number of moiré unit cells and
\lambda=0.3376a_{M}
is the damping factor, where
a_{M}
is the moiré lattice constant.
The hybridization matrix
H^{(fc,\eta)}
has the form of

H^{(fc,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma\sigma_{0}+v_{\star}^{\prime}(\eta k_{x}\sigma_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{y}),&0_{2\times 2}\end{pmatrix}

(S4)

which describe the hybridization between
f
electrons and
\Gamma_{3}

c
electrons
(a=1,2)
. Parameter values are
\gamma=-24.75
meV,
v_{\star}^{\prime}=1.622\mathrm{eV}\cdot\mathrm{\mathring{A}}
.

{\hat{H}}_{U}
(
U=57.89\mathrm{meV}
) describes the on-site interactions of
f
-electrons.

{\hat{H}}_{U}=\frac{U}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:n^{f}_{\mathbf{R}}::n^{f}_{\mathbf{R}}:,

(S5)

where
n^{f}_{\mathbf{R}}=\sum_{\alpha\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}
is the
f
-electrons density and the colon symbols represent the normal ordered operator with respect to the normal state:
:f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\alpha_{1}\eta_{1}s_{1}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha_{2}\eta_{2}s_{2}}:=f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\alpha_{1}\eta_{1}s_{1}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha_{2}\eta_{2}s_{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\alpha_{1}\eta_{1}s_{1};\alpha_{2}\eta_{2}s_{2}}
.

The ferromagnetic exchange interaction between
f
and
c
electrons
{\hat{H}}_{J}
is defined as

\displaystyle H_{J}=-\frac{J}{2N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}s_{1}s_{2}}\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}_{1}|,|\mathbf{k}_{2}|<\Lambda_{c}}e^{i(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{R}}(\eta\eta^{\prime}+(-1)^{\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}}):f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s_{1}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}::c_{\mathbf{k}_{2},\alpha^{\prime}+2,\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}_{1},\alpha+2,\eta s_{1}}:

(S6)

where
J=16.38
meV and
:c_{\mathbf{k}_{2},\alpha^{\prime}+2,\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}_{1},\alpha+2,\eta s_{1}}:=c_{\mathbf{k}_{2},\alpha^{\prime}+2,\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}_{1},\alpha+2,\eta s_{1}}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2}}\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{s_{1},s_{2}}

The repulsion between
f
and
c
electrons
{\hat{H}}_{W}
has the form of

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{W}=\sum_{\eta,s,\eta^{\prime},s^{\prime},a,\alpha}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},|\bm{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}}|<\Lambda_{c}}W_{a}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{R}}:f_{\mathbf{R},a\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},a\eta s}::c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},a\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:

(S7)

where
W_{1}=W_{2},W_{3}=W_{4}

Song and Bernevig 2022
. We further require
W_{1}=W_{2}=W_{3}=W_{4}=W=47
meV (the difference is about 10-15% )
.

The Coulomb interaction between
c
electrons has the form of

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{V}=\frac{1}{2\Omega_{0}N_{M}}\sum_{\eta_{1}s_{1}a_{1}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a_{2}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}_{1}|,|\mathbf{k}_{2}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{q}\\
|\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{q}|,|\mathbf{k}_{2}+\mathbf{q}|<\Lambda_{c}\end{subarray}}V(\mathbf{q}):c_{\mathbf{k}_{1}a_{1}\eta_{1}s_{1}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{q}a_{1}\eta_{1}s_{1}}::c_{\mathbf{k}_{2}+\mathbf{q}a_{2}\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}_{2}a_{2}\eta_{2}s_{2}}:

(S8)

where
\Omega_{0}
is the area of the moiré unit cell and
V(\mathbf{q}=0)/\Omega_{0}=48.33\mathrm{meV}
.

We will always take a mean-field treatment of
{\hat{H}}_{V}
, which gives

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}=-\frac{V(0)}{2\Omega_{0}}N_{M}\nu_{c}^{2}+\frac{V(0)}{\Omega_{0}}\nu_{c}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},a\eta s}(c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta s}-1/2)

(S9)

where
\nu_{c}
is the filling of
c
-electrons and
|\psi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\nu_{c}=\langle\psi_{0}|\hat{\nu_{c}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\,.

(S10)

|\psi_{0}\rangle
is the ground state and the density operator
\hat{\nu_{c}}
of
c
-electrons is defined

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{c}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},a\eta s}c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta s}-1/2

(S11)

and
|\psi_{0}\rangle
is the ground state.
Clearly, at the mean-field level
{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
is equivalent to a chemical potential shifting. In addition, the energy loss from
{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
is

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{0}|{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}|\psi_{0}\rangle=\frac{V(0)}{2\Omega_{0}}N_{M}\nu_{c}^{2}

(S12)

The model has a
U(4)\times U(4)
symmetry at chiral-flat limit
M=0,v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
, a flat
U(4)
symmetry at flat limit
M=0
and a chiral
U(4)
symmetry at
v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
. In addition, the particle-hole conjugation transformation will map the ground state of the model at
\nu
(total filling of
f
and
c
electrons) to the ground state at
-\nu
. Thus we only consider
\nu\leq 0
in this work.

S1.1
U(4)
moments

To observe the symmetry of the system, we introduce the following flat
U(4)
moments
(flat
U(4)
symmetry at
M=0
)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}(\mathbf{R})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{\alpha-1}\eta}}{2}A^{\mu\nu}_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}

(S13)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}(\mathbf{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}}{2N_{M}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}A^{\mu\nu}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}},\;(a,a^{\prime}=1,2)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}(\mathbf{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}}{2N_{M}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}B^{\mu\nu}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}},\;(a,a^{\prime}=3,4)

where repeated indices should be summed over and
A^{\mu\nu},B^{\mu\nu}
(
\mu,\nu=0,x,y,z
) are eight-by-eight matrices

\displaystyle A^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\{\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\}

(S14)

\displaystyle B^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\{\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},-\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},-\sigma_{y}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\}\ ,

with
\sigma_{0,x,y,z}
,
\tau_{0,x,y,z}
,
{\varsigma}_{0,x,y,z}
being the Pauli or identity matrices for the orbital, valley, and spin degrees of freedom, respectively.
The
\pm 1
valued index
\xi
, equal to
(-1)^{\alpha-1}\eta
or
(-1)^{a-1}\eta
in the
generators the
f
and
c
electrons respectively
, labels different fundamental representations of the flat-U(4) group.
The global flat-U(4) rotations are generated by

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}=\sum_{\xi=\pm 1}\bigg({\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}\bigg).

(S15)

The chiral
U(4)
moments
(chiral limit with
v_{\star}^{\prime}=0
and different other parameters
Song and Bernevig 2022
)
can be defined in a similar manner

\displaystyle{\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}(\mathbf{R})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{\alpha-1}\eta}}{2}\Theta^{\mu\nu,f}_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}

(S16)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}(\mathbf{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}}{2N_{M}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\Theta^{\mu\nu,c^{\prime}}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}},\;(a,a^{\prime}=1,2)

\displaystyle{\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}(\mathbf{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{\xi,(-1)^{a-1}\eta}}{2N_{M}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\Theta^{\mu\nu,c^{\prime\prime}}_{a\eta s,a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}},\;(a,a^{\prime}=3,4)

where
\mu,\nu=0,x,y,z
, and the repeated indices should be summed over. In addition,

\Theta^{(0\nu,f)}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Theta^{(0\nu,c^{\prime})}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\qquad\Theta^{(0\nu,c^{\prime\prime})}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\ ,

\Theta^{(x\nu,f)}=\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Theta^{(x\nu,c^{\prime})}=\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\qquad\Theta^{(x\nu,c^{\prime\prime})}=-\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\ ,

\Theta^{(y\nu,f)}=\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Theta^{(y\nu,c^{\prime})}=\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\qquad\Theta^{(y\nu,c^{\prime\prime})}=-\sigma_{x}\tau_{y}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\ ,

\Theta^{(z\nu,f)}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu},\qquad\Theta^{(z\nu,c^{\prime})}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\qquad\Theta^{(z\nu,c^{\prime\prime})}=\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}{\varsigma}_{\nu}\ .

The global chiral-
U(4)
rotations are generated by
{\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}=\sum_{\xi=\pm 1}\bigg({\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}+{\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime,\xi)}+{\hat{\Theta}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}\bigg)
.

The relations between chiral and flat
U(4)
moments are

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{0\nu}^{f,\xi}(\mathbf{R})={\hat{\Theta}}_{0\nu}^{f,\xi}(\mathbf{R})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{z\nu}^{f,\xi}(\mathbf{R})={\hat{\Theta}}_{z\nu}^{f,\xi}(\mathbf{R})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{x\nu}^{f,\xi}(\mathbf{R})=\xi{\hat{\Theta}}_{y\nu}^{(f,\xi)}(\mathbf{R})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{y\nu}^{f,\xi}(\mathbf{R})=-\xi{\hat{\Theta}}_{x\nu}^{(f,\xi)}(\mathbf{R})

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{0\nu}^{c^{\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{R})={\hat{\Theta}}_{0\nu}^{c^{\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{z\nu}^{c^{\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})={\hat{\Theta}}_{z\nu}^{c^{\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{x\nu}^{c^{\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})=\xi{\hat{\Theta}}_{y\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi)}(\mathbf{q})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{y\nu}^{c^{\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})=-\xi{\hat{\Theta}}_{x\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi)}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{0\nu}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})={\hat{\Theta}}_{0\nu}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{z\nu}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})={\hat{\Theta}}_{z\nu}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{x\nu}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})=\xi{\hat{\Theta}}_{y\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi)}(\mathbf{q})\quad\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{y\nu}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}(\mathbf{q})=-\xi{\hat{\Theta}}_{x\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi)}(\mathbf{q})

(S17)

We note that even though two
U(4)
moments are related via Eq.
S17
, they actually characterize two different
U(4)
symmetries.

S1.2
\psi
basis and
U(8)
moments

In this section, we introduce more general
U(8)
moments, where 8=2(orbital)
\times
2(valley)
\times
2(spin).

We first consider the following convenient basis of electron operators

\psi^{f,\xi}_{\mathbf{R},n},\psi^{c^{\prime},\xi}_{\mathbf{k},n},\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}_{\mathbf{k},n}

(
n=1,2,3,4
,
\xi=\pm
):

\displaystyle(\psi^{f,+}_{\mathbf{R},1},\psi^{f,+}_{\mathbf{R},2},\psi^{f,+}_{\mathbf{R},3},\psi^{f,+}_{\mathbf{R},4})=(f_{\mathbf{R},1+\uparrow},f_{\mathbf{R},2-\uparrow},f_{\mathbf{R},1+\downarrow},f_{\mathbf{R},2-\downarrow})\quad

\displaystyle(\psi^{f,-}_{\mathbf{R},1},\psi^{f,-}_{\mathbf{R},2},\psi^{f,-}_{\mathbf{R},3},\psi^{f,-}_{\mathbf{R},4})=(f_{\mathbf{R},2+\uparrow},-f_{\mathbf{R},1-\uparrow}f_{\mathbf{R},2+\downarrow},-f_{\mathbf{R},1-\downarrow})

\displaystyle(\psi^{c^{\prime},+}_{\mathbf{k},1},\psi^{c^{\prime},+}_{\mathbf{k},2},\psi^{c^{\prime},+}_{\mathbf{k},3},\psi^{c^{\prime},+}_{\mathbf{k},4})=(c_{\mathbf{k},1+\uparrow},c_{\mathbf{k},2-\uparrow},c_{\mathbf{k},1+\downarrow},c_{\mathbf{k},2-\downarrow})

\displaystyle(\psi^{c^{\prime},-}_{\mathbf{k},1},\psi^{c^{\prime},-}_{\mathbf{k},2},\psi^{c^{\prime},-}_{\mathbf{k},3},\psi^{c^{\prime},-}_{\mathbf{k},4})=(c_{\mathbf{k},2+\uparrow},-c_{\mathbf{k},1-\uparrow},c_{\mathbf{k},2+\downarrow},-c_{\mathbf{k},1-\downarrow})

\displaystyle(\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},+}_{\mathbf{k},1},\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},+}_{\mathbf{k},2},\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},+}_{\mathbf{k},3},\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},+}_{\mathbf{k},4})=(c_{\mathbf{k},3+\uparrow},-c_{\mathbf{k},4-\uparrow},c_{\mathbf{k},3+\downarrow},-c_{\mathbf{k},4-\downarrow})\quad

\displaystyle(\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},-}_{\mathbf{k},1},\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},-}_{\mathbf{k},2},\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},-}_{\mathbf{k},3},\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},-}_{\mathbf{k},4})=(c_{\mathbf{k},4+\uparrow},c_{\mathbf{k},3-\uparrow},c_{\mathbf{k},4+\downarrow},c_{\mathbf{k},3-\downarrow})

(S18)

Here, the index
\xi=\eta(-1)^{\alpha+1}

can be understood as the index of Chern basis
Bernevig et al. 2021a
.

The single-particle Hamiltonian
{\hat{H}}_{c}
(Eq.
S2
) and
{\hat{H}}_{fc}
(Eq.
S2
) in the
\psi
basis takes the form of

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{c}=

\displaystyle\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},n,\xi}(\Psi_{\mathbf{k}}^{c,\xi})^{{\dagger}}v_{\star}\begin{bmatrix}0_{4\times 4}&(k_{x}+i\xi k_{y})\mathbb{I}_{4\times 4}\\
(k_{x}-i\xi k_{y})\mathbb{I}_{4\times 4}&0_{4\times 4}\end{bmatrix}\Psi_{\mathbf{k}}^{c,\xi}+\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},n,\xi}(-1)^{n+1}M\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}^{c^{\prime\prime},-\xi}

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{fc}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{M}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},\mathbf{R},n\end{subarray}}\bigg(e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}-\frac{|\mathbf{k}|^{2}\lambda^{2}}{2}}\tilde{H}_{\xi\xi^{\prime}}^{(fc)}(\mathbf{k})\psi_{\mathbf{R},n}^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}^{c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}+\text{h.c.}\bigg),\quad\tilde{H}^{(fc)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{bmatrix}\gamma&v_{\star}^{\prime}k_{-}\\
v_{\star}^{\prime}k_{+}&\gamma\end{bmatrix}

(S19)

where
\Psi^{c,\xi}_{\mathbf{k}}=\begin{bmatrix}\psi_{\mathbf{k},1,...,4}^{c^{\prime},\xi}&\psi_{\mathbf{k},1,...,4}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}\end{bmatrix}^{T}
and
k_{\pm}=k_{x}\pm ik_{y}
.

We now define the
U(8)
moments with
\psi
basis (
\mu,\nu=0,x,y,z
):

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{mn}\psi^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}_{\mathbf{R},n}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{nm}\psi^{f,\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{R},m}

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{mn}\psi^{c^{\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},n}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{nm}\psi^{c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k},m}\quad,\quad{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{mn}\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},n}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{nm}\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k},m}

\displaystyle\{T^{\mu\nu}\}=\{\varsigma^{\prime}_{\nu}\rho_{0},\varsigma^{\prime}_{\nu}\rho_{y},-\varsigma^{\prime}_{\nu}\rho_{x},\varsigma^{\prime}_{\nu}\rho_{z}\}

(S20)

and we let
\rho_{x,y,z,0}
be the Pauli matrices and the identity matrix defined in the subspace of
(1+,2-)
for
\xi=+1
and
(2+,1-)
for
\xi=-1
.
\varsigma^{\prime}_{x,y,z,0}
are the Pauli matrices and identity matrix acting in the spin subspace.

The previous flat
U(4)
moments in Eq.
S16
can be written with new electron basis as

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}(\mathbf{R})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{mn}\psi^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}_{\mathbf{R},n}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{nm}\psi^{f,\xi}_{\mathbf{R},m}={\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi)}(\mathbf{R})

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi)}(\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2N_{M}}\sum_{mn,\mathbf{k}}\psi^{c^{\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},n}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{nm}\psi^{c^{\prime},\xi}_{\mathbf{k},m}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi\xi)}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi)}(\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2N_{M}}\sum_{mn}\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},n}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{nm}\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}_{\mathbf{k},m}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi\xi)}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\,.

(S21)

The advantage of using the
\psi
basis is that the flat
U(4)
moments of
f,c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}
electrons have the same matrix structure
T^{\mu\nu}
.

It is also useful to consider the following Fourier transformation of electron operators and
f
-moments.

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathbf{r},n}^{c^{\prime},\xi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{M}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}^{c^{\prime},\xi}

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathbf{r},n}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{M}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi}

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}-i(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\mathbf{r}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{mn}\psi_{\mathbf{r},n}^{c^{\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{nm}\psi_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},m}^{c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}^{\prime}-i(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})\mathbf{r}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{mn}\psi_{\mathbf{r},n}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{nm}\psi_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},m}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime}}\,.

(S22)

Since the momentum of
c
-electron has a finite cutoff, this definition is for convenience. However, if we only consider the
c
-electrons in the first moiré Brillouin zone, the completeness of
c
-electron Fourier transformation is guaranteed and a well-defined inverse Fourier transformation also exists for
c
electrons. For what follows, unless specifically mentioned, we only consider
c
-electrons in the first moiré Brillouin zone.

Now, we utilize the following relation

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{ab}[T^{\mu\nu}]_{cd}=4\delta_{a,d}\delta_{b,c}

(S23)

and find

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R}){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi_{2}^{\prime}\xi_{2})}(\mathbf{R}_{2})=\sum_{a,b}\psi_{\mathbf{R},a}^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{R},b}^{f,\xi^{\prime}}\psi_{\mathbf{R}_{2},b}^{f,\xi^{\prime}_{2},{\dagger}}\psi^{f,\xi_{2}}_{\mathbf{R}_{2},a}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{r}_{1},\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime}){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime}\xi_{2})}(\mathbf{r}_{2}^{\prime},\mathbf{r}_{2})=\sum_{a,b}\psi_{\mathbf{r}_{1},a}^{c^{\prime},\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{r}_{1}^{\prime},b}^{c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}}\psi_{\mathbf{r}_{2}^{\prime},b}^{c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}_{2},{\dagger}}\psi^{c^{\prime},\xi_{2}}_{\mathbf{r}_{2},a}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R}){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi_{2}^{\prime}\xi_{2})}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\sum_{a,b,\mathbf{R}}\psi_{\mathbf{R},a}^{f,\xi,{\dagger}}\psi_{\mathbf{R},b}^{f,\xi^{\prime}}\psi_{\mathbf{r},b}^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi^{\prime}_{2},{\dagger}}\psi^{c^{\prime\prime},\xi_{2}}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},a}

(S24)

We change from
\psi
basis to
f
and
c
basis,
then Eq.
S24
becomes (which will be used in Sec.
S4
)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R}){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{R}_{2})=\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}ss^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}.f_{\mathbf{R}_{2},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}_{2},\alpha\eta s}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R}){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}ss^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}.c_{\mathbf{r},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\alpha\eta s}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R}){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,-\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{R}_{2})=\sum_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R}_{2},\alpha_{2}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}_{2},\alpha\eta s}\eta^{\prime}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R}){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},-\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{r},\alpha_{2}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\alpha\eta s}\eta^{\prime}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R}){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},-\xi^{\prime}-\xi)}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{r},\alpha_{2}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime}\eta s}\eta^{\prime}\eta[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{2}}[\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha_{2}^{\prime},\alpha}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})\xi^{\prime}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},-\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{r},\alpha_{2}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\alpha\eta s}[i\sigma_{y}]_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}(-\xi){\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})\xi^{\prime}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},-\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{r},\alpha_{2}^{\prime}\eta s}[i\sigma_{y}]_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{2}}[i\sigma_{y}]_{\alpha_{2}^{\prime},\alpha}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})\xi^{\prime}{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},-\xi^{\prime}\xi)}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime})=\sum_{\alpha\eta s,\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{r},\alpha_{2}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime}\eta s}[i\sigma_{y}]_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha_{2}}[\eta\sigma_{x}]_{\alpha_{2}^{\prime},\alpha}

(S25)

S2
Zero-hybridziation limit

The
f
-
c
hybridization
parameter
\gamma
vanishes at
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.9
which is close to the actual value
w_{0}/w_{1}=0.8
. Vanishing
\gamma
corresponds to the gap closing between the remote and the flat bands in the continuum single particle model. We solve the model exactly at
\gamma=0
and then treat it as a perturbation.
In this limit, the
f
and
c
electrons are coupled only through the interacting terms.
We now neglect
{\hat{H}}_{V}
except for a mean-field treatment- for the same reason as in Ref.
Song and Bernevig 2022
, that it will cause only a velocity renormalization of the linear conduction fermion, and keep only the
remaining
terms
{\hat{H}}_{U},{\hat{H}}_{W},{\hat{H}}_{J},{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
where
{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
denotes the
{\hat{H}}_{V}
with mean-field approximation.

This now becomes a polynomially solvable Hamiltonian. Some remarks:
1.
This is more complicated than the zero hybridization limit of the Anderson
lattice
model in which the Hilbert space factorizes. This is because in both
{\hat{H}}_{W}

(we have work in the limit
W_{1}=W_{2}=W_{3}=W_{4}
)
, and
{\hat{H}}_{J}
terms, the
f
occupation number will influence the electron Hamiltonian, but in a one-body fashion, since the
c
operators are quadratic.
The strategy is then to solve the Hamiltonian in this limit and then add the
f
-
c
hybridization perturbatively
via Schrieffer–Wolff transformation (SW) transformation
Schrieffer and Wolff 1966
.

To solve the Hamiltonian we first need to find the on-site configurations of the
f
fermions (which are good quantum numbers) which then, after adding
{\hat{H}}_{U}+{\hat{H}}_{W}+{\hat{H}}_{J}
and the
c
-electron kinetic term

\displaystyle H_{c}=\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{aa^{\prime}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}(H^{(c,\eta)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})-\mu\delta_{aa^{\prime}})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta s}-\mu N_{f}

(S26)

give the lowest energy. This is a minimization problem similar in spirit to the Lieb theorems
Lieb 1994
with flux
\pi
(used in the Kitaev model
Kitaev 2006
) where it is shown that the flux
\pi
configuration of a noninteracting fermion model in a background plaquette flux with each plaquette having possibly different flux is minimal at flux
\pi
per all plaquettes. Our problem, at least in the first try, might be amenable to Monte Carlo sampling.

S2.1
Symmetries of the zero-hybridization Limit

We can rewrite the
{\hat{H}}_{U}+{\hat{H}}_{W}+{\hat{H}}_{J}

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{U}=\frac{U}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:n_{f\mathbf{R}}::n_{f\mathbf{R}},\;\;\;{\hat{H}}_{W}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:n_{f\mathbf{R}}:\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ ,

\displaystyle H_{J}=-\frac{J}{2N}\sum_{\mathbf{R}s_{1}s_{2}}\sum_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}_{1}|,|\mathbf{k}_{2}|<\Lambda_{c}}e^{i(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{k}_{2})\cdot\mathbf{R}}(\eta\eta^{\prime}+(-1)^{\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}}):f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s_{1}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}::c_{\mathbf{k}_{2},\alpha^{\prime}+2,\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}_{1},\alpha+2,\eta s_{1}}:

(S27)

Where we have defined the occupation number of the electron on-site
\mathbf{R}

n_{f\mathbf{R}}=\sum_{\alpha\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s}\in 0,1\ldots 8

(S28)

which is a symmetry of the system
even when
W_{1}\neq W_{3}
:

[{\hat{H}}_{U},n_{f\mathbf{R}}]=0,\;\;[{\hat{H}}_{W},n_{f\mathbf{R}}]=0,\;\;[{\hat{H}}_{J},n_{f\mathbf{R}}]=0,\;\;[H_{c},n_{f\mathbf{R}}]=0

(S29)

The system has a large
U(1)^{N_{M}}
symmetry where
N_{M}
is the number of moiré unit cells. An eigenstate of the system is first indexed by
n_{f\mathbf{R}}
. Then this couples through the
{\hat{H}}_{W}
and (with a smaller coefficient) through
{\hat{H}}_{J}
.

There are now several questions

•

If first we neglect
{\hat{H}}_{J}
we have a large symmetry,
U(8)^{N_{M}}
, whose generators are
{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})={\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})+{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(c^{\prime\prime},\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})
(Eq.
S20
).

[{\hat{H}}_{U},{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})]=0\quad,\quad[{\hat{H}}_{W},{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})]=0\quad,\quad[H_{c},{\hat{\Sigma}}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi\xi^{\prime})}(\mathbf{R})]=0

(S30)

which gives
huge number
of degenerate states, all with the same occupation number
n_{f\mathbf{R}}
on-site distributed around the different
\alpha,\eta,s.

•

If we take
W_{1}=W_{3}=W
then we have that

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{W}=W\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:n_{f\mathbf{R}}:\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}\sum_{\eta sa}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta s}:

\displaystyle=W\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:n_{f\mathbf{R}}:\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}(\sum_{a}U^{\eta,*}_{\mathbf{k},an_{1}}U^{\eta,*}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},an_{2}})\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n_{1}\eta s}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n_{2}\eta s}

(S31)

where we have introduced the eigenvectors
U^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},an}
and eigenvalues
\epsilon_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\eta}
of
{\hat{H}}^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle\sum_{a^{\prime}}H^{c}_{aa^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})U^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k},a^{\prime}n}=\epsilon^{\eta}_{k,n}U_{\mathbf{k},an}\,,

and the operator in the band basis

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n\eta s}=\sum_{a}U_{\mathbf{k},an}^{*}c_{\mathbf{k},a\eta s}\,.

{\hat{H}}_{W}
is now a one-body term for
c
or
\gamma
, which depends on the distribution of
n_{\mathbf{R}}^{f}
. Through Monte Carlo sampling, we can find the ground-state exactly and check whether the ground state involve uniform
n_{\mathbf{R}}^{f}
or not.

•

If furthermore, we assume uniform
n^{f}_{\mathbf{R}}=n_{f}
then the summation over
\mathbf{R}
gives us a
\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}
and we obtain an
n^{f}
-dependent chemical potential of the electrons:

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{W}=Wn_{f}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{n\eta s}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}

(S32)

We can now find easily the admixture of
f,c
fermions at any filling
N=N_{f}+N_{c}
with
N_{f}=N_{M}n_{f}
. This allows us to see, as a function of the total filling, analytically, the distribution between the
f,c
electrons
in the ground state
. We can then add the
{\hat{H}}_{J}
under the stronger assumption that

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\eta s_{1}}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}=A_{\alpha\eta s_{1},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}\,.

(S33)

We can then find the
A_{\alpha\eta s_{1},\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s_{2}}
which will minimize the state energy and break the
U(8)
on-site symmetry to
U(1)
symmetry
.

•

We can assume
n_{\mathbf{R}}^{f}
not constant, and
adopt
charge density wave (CDW) or other types of translational symmetry breaking.
We leave it for future study

•

We can then add the
f^{\dagger}c
term through Schrieffer-Wollf
(SW) transformation
,
which will be discussed in the Sec.
S4
.

Figure S1:
Zero hybridization
\gamma=0
model at different fillings of the Heavy fermions (the state assumed as the parent state carries all the filling in the heavy fermions, unlike the exact state at
\gamma=0
which contains both
c
and
f
fermions). The Fermi level is a horizontal dashed black line. The solid lines are zero hybridization scenarios while the green dashed lines are the level splittings occurring after small increase of
\gamma
from nonzero, but still smaller than the realistic value of
\gamma=24.6
meV
. At
\nu=0
both minima of the bands are made by
c
electrons. At
\nu=-1+\epsilon
the band is very flat while at
\nu=-1-\epsilon
the minimum of the band is formed by the
c
electrons. This last situation changes at
\nu=-2,-3
, at least in the absence of hybridization: both band edges are made up of heavy fermions. At small hybridization (green dashed) the band edges at
\nu=-2\pm\epsilon
and
\nu=-3\pm\epsilon
are still made up by heavy fermions. Once
\gamma
reaches its large
24.6
meV
value, we find that at
\nu=-2-\epsilon
the band has changed character from heavy fermion to light fermion due to large heavy-light mixing (see violet dashed line, large curvature). At
\nu=-3-\epsilon
however, the portion of the heavy band as a ratio of the Brillouin zone is larger and hence upon hybridization, the portion of the mixing is smaller than at
\nu=-2-\epsilon
, and the band has more
f
-character. The CDW
Xie et al. 2022
obtained at
\nu=-3
most likely has
f
-
f
CDW correlations between the
\nu=-3+\epsilon
band edge (which is around
K,M

points
and hence made up of heavy fermions) and the
\nu=-3-\epsilon
band edge (around
\Gamma
but still made up of heavy fermions)

S2.2
Zero hybridization limit without
{\hat{H}}_{J}

We first solve the zero hybridization model at
J=0
.
The total Hamiltonian of the zero-hybridization model reads

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{c}+\hat{H}_{U}+\hat{H}_{W}+\hat{H}_{V}^{MF}

(S34)

We consider the solution with uniform charge distribution
of
f
-electrons
n_{\mathbf{R}}^{f}=\nu_{f}+4
, with integer
\nu_{f}\in[-4,...,4]
. We note that, in the zero-hybridization model, the filling of
f
-electron of each site is a good quantum number and takes integer values.
.
The trial wavefunction of the ground state we proposed is

\displaystyle|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle=[\prod_{\mathbf{R}}|\nu_{f}\rangle_{\mathbf{R}}]|\Psi[\nu-\nu_{f},\nu_{f}]\rangle_{c}.

(S35)

where
\nu_{f}
denotes the filling of
f
electrons,
\nu
denotes the total filling of
f
and
c
electrons, and
\nu_{c}=\nu-\nu_{f}
is the filling of
c
electrons.

•

|\nu_{f}\rangle_{\mathbf{R}}
describes a
f
state at site
\mathbf{R}
with filling
\nu_{f}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\eta s}:f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}:|\nu_{f}\rangle_{\mathbf{R}}=\nu_{f}|\nu_{f}\rangle_{\mathbf{R}}.

and we take a uniform charge distribution, so for each site, the fillings of
f
-electrons are the same.

•

|\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}]\rangle_{s}
denotes a Slater determinant state corresponding to the ground state of the following one-body Hamiltonian of
c
electrons at filling
\nu_{c}=\nu-\nu_{f}
:

\displaystyle H_{c}^{\prime}=\hat{H}_{c}+\sum_{\eta,s,a}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}W\nu_{f}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}:.

Above one-body Hamiltonian can be diagonalized.
(Note that
W_{a}
is taken to be orbital independent):

\displaystyle H_{c}^{\prime}=\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c},\eta,s,n}(E_{\mathbf{k}n\eta}+W\nu_{f})\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta}

where
n
is the band index and
\gamma
is the operator in the band basis

\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}=\sum_{a}U^{\eta,*}_{\mathbf{k}an}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s},\;\;\;c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}=\sum_{n}U^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}an}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s},\;\;\;\sum_{a^{\prime}}H^{(c,\eta)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})U^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}n}=E_{\mathbf{k}n\eta}U^{\eta}_{\mathbf{k}an}

(S36)

The energy of
|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle
state is then

\displaystyle E_{\nu_{f},\nu}/N_{M}=\langle\nu_{f},\nu|{\hat{H}}_{U}|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle/N_{M}+\langle\nu_{f},\nu|{\hat{H}}_{V}|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle/N_{M}+\langle\nu_{f},\nu|{\hat{H}}_{W}|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle/N_{M}+\langle\nu_{f},\nu|{\hat{H}}_{c}|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle/N_{M}

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{f},\nu|{\hat{H}}_{U}|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle/N_{M}=\frac{U}{2}\nu_{f}^{2}\quad,\quad\langle\nu_{f},\nu|{\hat{H}}^{MF}_{V}|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle/N_{M}=\frac{V_{0}}{2\Omega_{0}}\nu_{c}^{2}=\frac{V_{0}}{2\Omega_{0}}(\nu-\nu_{f})^{2}

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{f},\nu|{\hat{H}}_{W}|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle/N_{M}=W\nu_{f}\nu_{c}=W\nu_{f}(\nu-\nu_{f})

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{f},\nu|{\hat{H}}_{c}|\nu_{f},\nu\rangle/N_{M}=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},n\eta s}(E_{\mathbf{k}n\eta})\langle\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}|\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}]\rangle

\displaystyle E_{\nu_{f},\nu}/N_{M}=\frac{U_{1}}{2}\nu_{f}^{2}+\frac{V_{0}}{2\Omega_{0}}(\nu-\nu_{f})^{2}+W\nu_{f}(\nu-\nu_{f})+\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},n\eta s}(E_{\mathbf{k}n\eta})\langle\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}|\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}]\rangle

For a given total filling
\nu
, we compare the energies of different
\nu_{f}
(
\nu_{f}
can only be an integer) and take the one with the lowest energy as our ground state.

In the
M=0
limit, the analytical expression of
E_{\nu_{f},\nu}/N_{M}
can be easily given At
M=0
,
c
electron dispersion becomes
\pm v_{\star}|k|
. Filling
\nu_{c}=\nu-\nu_{f}
conduction electrons is equivalent to fill the 8-fold (
8=2\times 2\times 2
, 2 for spin, 2 for valley, 2 for orbital) linear-dispersive bands up to certain momentum
k_{0}
.
Depending
on the sign of
\nu_{c}
, we either
fill
electrons (
\nu_{c}>0
) or holes
(\nu_{c}<0)
to the Dirac sea.
This gives the following equations to determine
k_{0}

\displaystyle\frac{8}{A_{MBZ}}\int_{|\mathbf{k}|<k_{0}}dk_{x}dk_{y}=|\nu_{c}|\Rightarrow k_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{|\nu_{c}|A_{MBZ}}{8\pi}}

(S37)

where
A_{MBZ}
is the size of moiré Brillouin zone. The energy
loss
from conduction bands are then (factor 8 for 8-fold degenerate bands)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},n\eta s}(E_{\mathbf{k}n\eta})\langle\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}|\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}]\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{8}{A_{MBZ}}\int_{|\mathbf{k}|<k_{0}}|v_{\star}||\mathbf{k}|dk_{x}dk_{y}=\frac{16}{A_{MBZ}}\pi|v_{\star}|\frac{k_{0}^{3}}{3}

Finally, we have

\displaystyle E_{\nu_{f},\nu}/N_{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{U_{1}}{2}\nu_{f}^{2}+\frac{V_{0}}{2\Omega_{0}}(\nu-\nu_{f})^{2}+W\nu_{f}(\nu-\nu_{f})+\frac{16\pi|v_{\star}|k_{0}^{3}}{3A_{MBZ}}

\displaystyle=\frac{U_{1}}{2}\nu_{f}^{2}+\frac{V_{0}}{2\Omega_{0}}(\nu-\nu_{f})^{2}+W\nu_{f}(\nu-\nu_{f})+\frac{16\pi|v_{\star}|}{3A_{MBZ}}\bigg[\frac{|\nu-\nu_{f}|A_{MBZ}}{8\pi}\bigg]^{3/2}

(S38)

The behaviors of
E_{\nu_{f},\nu}/N_{M}
as a function of
\nu
at various
\nu_{f}
is shown in Fig.
S2
.

We next consider non-zero
M
. Now the dispersions of conduction electrons become
\frac{\pm M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}+4|v_{\star}|^{2}|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}{2}
. Without loss of generality, we consider the hole doping and fill the bands with energy
E<\mu
with
\mu<0
. Then the relation between
\nu_{c}
and
\mu
is

\displaystyle\nu_{c}=

\displaystyle\frac{-4}{A_{MBZ}}\int_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda}\theta(|\mu|-\frac{-M+\sqrt{M^{2}+4|v_{\star}|^{2}|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}{2})dk_{x}dk_{y}

\displaystyle+\frac{-4}{A_{MBZ}}\int_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda}\theta(|\mu|-\frac{M+\sqrt{M^{2}+4|v_{\star}|^{2}|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}{2})dk_{x}dk_{y}

(S39)

(where
4
comes from 2 valleys and 2 spins, and we fill the hole bands with energy
E>-\mu
). For
|\mu|<M
, we find

\displaystyle\nu_{c}=-\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{|\mu|(|\mu|+M)}{|v_{\star}|^{2}}

(S40)

For
|\mu|>M
, we find

\displaystyle\nu_{c}=-\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{|\mu|(|\mu|+M)}{|v_{\star}|^{2}}-\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{|\mu|(|\mu|-M)}{|v_{\star}|^{2}}

(S41)

Combining Eq.
S40
and Eq.
S41
, we find

\displaystyle\nu_{c}=-\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{|\mu|(|\mu|+M)}{|v_{\star}|^{2}}-\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{|\mu|(|\mu|-M)}{|v_{\star}|^{2}}\theta(|\mu|-M)

(S42)

Then the energy loss from conduction
c
electron is

\displaystyle E_{c}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},n\eta s}(E_{\mathbf{k}n\eta})\langle\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}|\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}^{\dagger}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}n\eta s}|\Psi[\nu_{c},\nu_{f}]\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{A_{MBZ}}\int_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\theta(|\mu|-\frac{-M+\sqrt{M^{2}+4|v_{\star}|^{2}|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}{2})\frac{-M+\sqrt{M^{2}+4|v_{\star}|^{2}|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}{2}dk_{x}dk_{y}

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{A_{MBZ}}\int_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda}\theta(|\mu|-\frac{M+\sqrt{M^{2}+4|v_{\star}|^{2}|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}{2})\frac{M+\sqrt{M^{2}+4|v_{\star}|^{2}|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}}{2}dk_{x}dk_{y}

(S43)

For
|\mu|<M
, we find

\displaystyle E_{c}=\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{|\mu|(|\mu|+M)}{|v_{\star}|^{2}}

(S44)

For
|\mu|>M
, we find

\displaystyle E_{c}=\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{\mu^{2}+M|\mu|}{|v_{\star}|^{2}}+\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{|\mu|(|\mu|-M)}{|v_{\star}^{2}|}

(S45)

Combing Eq.
S44
and Eq.
S45
, we have

\displaystyle E_{c}=\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{\mu^{2}+M|\mu|}{|v_{\star}|^{2}}+\frac{4\pi}{A_{MBZ}}\frac{|\mu|(|\mu|-M)}{|v_{\star}^{2}|}\theta(|\mu|-M)

(S46)

Then the energy of the system is

\displaystyle E_{\nu_{f},\nu}/N_{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{U_{1}}{2}\nu_{f}^{2}+\frac{V_{0}}{2\Omega_{0}}(\nu-\nu_{f})^{2}+W\nu_{f}(\nu-\nu_{f})+E_{c}

(S47)

where
E_{c}
is given in Eq.
S46
and depends on
\mu
, with
\mu
solved from Eq.
S42
.
Solutions at nonzero
M
are shown in Fig.
S3
.

We notice two rules:

•

At integer
\nu
, the states with
\nu_{f}=\nu
would minimize the energy.

•

\nu=-3
is very close to the transition point (see Fig.
S3
)
between
\nu_{f}=-3
and
\nu_{f}=-2
.

We will consider
\nu=0,-1,-2
in this work. The
\nu=-3
needs separate treatment due to it being near the transition point between
\nu_{f}=-2,-3
. We point out that the
\nu=-3
case is also known to exhibit a competition of orders
Xie et al. 2022
.

Figure S2:

Evolution of energy as a function of
\nu
at different
\nu_{f}
and
M=0
. For fixed
\nu
, the ground state is determined by
\nu_{f}
that gives the lowest energy.

Figure S3:

Evolution of the
f
and
c
filling as a function of
\nu
. We observe that
\nu=-3
is near the transition point between
\nu_{f}=-2,-3
states.

S2.3
Solution of the one
f
-electron problem above (or below) uniform integer filling

We now consider one more electron than integer filling. The state reads

|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle=f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}|\nu_{f}\rangle|c\rangle

(S48)

where
|\nu_{f}\rangle
is the state with
\nu_{f}+4
fermions per site and
|c\rangle
is the conduction electron ground state of
{\hat{H}}_{c}
(Eq.
S2
) with filling
\nu_{c}=0
. We then have:

\sum_{\alpha}:f_{\mathbf{R}_{1},\alpha}^{\dagger}f_{\mathbf{R}_{1},\alpha}:|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle=(\delta_{\mathbf{R}_{1},\mathbf{R}}+\nu_{f})|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle

(S49)

(here
\alpha=1\ldots 8
over all valleys and spins and flavors)

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{U}|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{U}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:n_{f\mathbf{R}}::n_{f\mathbf{R}}:|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle=(N_{M}\nu_{f}^{2}\frac{U}{2}+\frac{U}{2}(1+2\nu_{f}))|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{W}|R,\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:n_{f\mathbf{R}}:\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ |\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}(\delta_{R_{1},R}+\nu_{f})\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ |\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu_{f}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ |\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot R}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ |\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle

This gives the one electron Hamiltonian for the
c
-electrons without the
ferromagnetic exchange interaction term
{\hat{H}}_{J}
(Eq.
S6
). The Coulomb repulsion
{\hat{H}}_{V}
has been treated at the mean-field level (Eq.
S9
) and is absorbed by the chemical potential of conduction
c
-electrons
.

\displaystyle H|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle

(S51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(N_{M}\nu_{f}^{2}\frac{U}{2}+\frac{U}{2}(1+2\nu_{f}))|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle+(\sum_{\eta s}\sum_{aa^{\prime}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}(H^{(c,\eta)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})-\mu\delta_{aa^{\prime}}+\nu_{f}W_{a}\delta_{aa^{\prime}})c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}a^{\prime}\eta s}+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ )|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(N_{M}\nu_{f}^{2}\frac{U}{2}+\frac{U}{2}(1+2\nu_{f}))|\mathbf{R},\alpha\rangle+\sum_{\eta s,\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\sum_{a,a^{\prime}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}(A_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}+B_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R}))c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}

We see that the Hamiltonian action on the one
f
particle above the integer state contains two
c
-electron terms. First:

A_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}=(H^{(c,\eta)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})-\mu\delta_{aa^{\prime}}+\nu_{f}W_{a}\delta_{aa^{\prime}})\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}

(S52)

has a continuous spectrum, given by
H^{(c,\eta)}_{a,a^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})
and shifted by an
a
-dependent chemical potential
W_{a}
. When
W_{1}=W_{2}
, the term is actually transformed into the density, and it is a bona-fide chemical potential. Let us call the eigenvalues of this term in Eq.
S52

\nu_{1}\geq\nu_{2}\geq\ldots\nu_{16N_{M}}
as the Hamiltonian has
16N_{M}
eigenvalues,
16 for each momentum
.

The second term is

B_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}({\mathbf{R}})=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}\delta_{a,a^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}

(S53)

Unfortunately Eq.
S52
and Eq.
S53
do not commute, hence obtaining the spectrum analytically is hard. However, we can still
derive clear theorems
. First we rewrite

B_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}})=\sum_{\eta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}a^{\prime\prime}}P_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s;\eta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}a^{\prime\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}})P^{\dagger}_{\eta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}a^{\prime\prime};\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a^{\prime};\eta;s^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}}),\;\;\;P_{\mathbf{k}a\eta s;\eta^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime\prime}a^{\prime\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}})=\sqrt{\frac{W_{a}}{N_{M}}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}\delta_{aa^{\prime\prime}}\delta_{\eta\eta^{\prime\prime}}\delta_{ss^{\prime\prime}}

(S54)

Since
B=P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}})P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}})^{\dagger}
, where
P{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}}
is a Rank
16
(
a=1\ldots 4,\eta=\pm,a=\pm
) matrix,
it is highly rank deficient and has a large number of zero eigenvalues. The nonzero eigenvalues are the same as those of the matrix
P^{\dagger}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}})P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}})
:

(P^{\dagger}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}})P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{R}}))_{\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}a^{\prime};\eta sa}=W_{a}\delta_{aa^{\prime}}\delta_{\eta\eta^{\prime}}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}

(S55)

where we have summed over all the momentum values
\mathbf{k}
in the first moiré Brillouin zone (i.e we took
\Lambda_{c}
to be entire first moiré Brillouin zone). Since
B
is a positive semidefinite matrix, we confirm all eigenvalues are non-negative. We now know that there are
16
nonzero eigenvalues,
8
of them being
W_{3}
, larger than the other
8
of them which are
W_{1}
. We now order them as such:
\rho_{1}=\ldots=\rho_{8}=W_{3}>\rho_{9}=\ldots=\rho_{16}=W_{1}>\rho_{17}=\ldots\ \rho_{16N_{M}}=0
.

The existence of only a finite (
N_{orbitals}=N_{orb}=16
) number of nonzero eigenvalues of the second matrix
B
with eigenvalues
\rho_{i}
and a continuum spectrum of eigenvalues of the first matrix
A
with eigenvalues
\nu_{i}
allows for strong theorems of the spectrum. We employ Weyl’s inequalities that say that the eigenvalues
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\mu_{N_{orb}N_{M}}
of the sum of two matrices
A,B
satisfy:

\nu_{j}+\rho_{k}\leq\mu_{i}\leq\nu_{r}+\rho_{s},\;\;\;\;\text{if}\;\;\;j+k-N_{orb}N_{M}\geq i\geq r+s-1

(S56)

For the
A,B
matric in Eq.
S52
and Eq.
S53
, we pick
k=N_{orb}N_{M}\implies\rho_{k}=0
and
s=N_{orb}+1\implies\rho_{s}=0
. We then have:

\nu_{j}\geq\mu_{i}\geq\nu_{r},\;\;\;\text{if}\;\;\;j\geq i\geq r+N_{orb}

(S57)

In Eq.
S56
, we pick
j=i,r=i-N_{orb}
and we have

\nu_{i}\leq\mu_{i}\leq\nu_{i-N_{orb}},\;\;\;i>N_{orb}

(S58)

Now we see that most of the
\mu
eigenvalues form a continuum themselves, as they are bounded between the eigenvalues of the matrix
A
, which form a continuum. In fact,
\mu_{N_{orb}+1}\geq\mu_{N_{orb}+2}\geq\ldots\geq\mu_{N_{orb}N_{M}}
are bounded by the eigenvalues of
A
,
\nu_{i},\nu_{i-N_{orb}}
; since these latter eigenvalues form a continuum, the most (most of the times they are degenerate as they represent equal energies in
k
-space, i.e. Fermi surfaces of the continuum Hamiltonian) they can have in energy separation is
N_{orb}v_{\star}2\pi/\sqrt{N_{M}}
where we have used the linear spectrum of
A
and the momentum quantum
2\pi/\sqrt{N_{M}}
. When
N_{M}\rightarrow\infty
the two eigenvalues are the same and hence to a good approximation
\mu_{i}\approx\nu_{i},\;\;i>N_{orb}
. (A similar proof is an observed reason why the particle-particle continuum is formed by sums of the particle-particle energies, and the bound/anti bound states form a finite number). The number of eigenvalues that can be away from a
\nu_{i}
is small,
\mu_{i},i=1,2\ldots N_{orb}
:
\nu_{i}\leq\mu_{i}\leq\nu_{i}+W
. These are anti-bound states (
i=1,2\ldots N_{orb}
and hence they are at the top of the spectrum, as they should be since
B

(Eq.
S53
)
is a positive semidefinite matrix.

Since the
c
-spectrum of the one-
f
fermion state in Eq.
S48
is unchanged except for the upper parts of the spectrum, adding one electron to the integer-filled heavy fermion ground-state at integer total fillings costs
\nu_{f}^{2}\frac{U}{2}+\frac{U}{2}(1+2\nu_{f})
per unit cell. As such it is beneficial to add
c
electrons, at least at
\nu_{f}=0
.

We next comment on the effect of
{\hat{H}}_{V}
. At the mean-field level (
{\hat{H}}_{V}\approx{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
, Eq.
S9
),
{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
corresponds to a chemical potential term. The
c
-spectrum remains gapless and forms a continuum even after including
{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
. Therefore, our statement remains valid.

S2.4
Charge density waves?

Consider charge neutrality
\nu_{f}=\nu_{c}=0
. As we showed, it costs
\frac{U}{2}
to add one
f
electron, hence upon changing the filling, it is easy to add
c
electrons.

Suppose we add
c
electron up to
k_{0}
defined in Eq.
S37
, adding one more
c
electron will cost

\displaystyle E_{c}=v_{\star}k_{0}+\frac{V(0)}{\Omega_{0}}\nu_{c}=v_{\star}k_{0}+\frac{V(0)}{\Omega_{0}}\nu_{c}=v_{\star}\sqrt{\frac{|\nu_{c}|A_{MBZ}}{8\pi}}+\frac{V(0)}{\Omega_{0}}\nu_{c}

(S59)

where the second term comes from the
{\hat{H}}_{V}^{MF}
(Eq.
S9
). For sufficient large
\nu_{c}
, we have
E_{c}>U_{1}/2
. Then
it becomes advantageous to add
f
-electrons, and they can be added (if we neglect
W
) from
:\nu_{f}:=0
all the way to
:\nu_{f}:=1
at
W=0
limit. At non-zero
W
, the process of adding electrons is not so straightforward.
We use a trial state

|\psi\rangle=\prod_{(\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s)\in S_{fill}}f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}|:\nu_{f}:=0\rangle|k_{0},\text{ c-filled}\rangle

(S60)

where
S_{fill}
is a set with
N_{M}\nu_{f}
elements, that characterize the site and flavor (orbital, valley, and spin)
where the
f
-electron is filled.
We have that:

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{W}|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}:n^{f}_{\mathbf{R}}:\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ |\psi\rangle

(S61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s}\sum_{(\mathbf{R}_{1},\alpha_{1}\eta_{1}s_{1})\in S_{fill}}\delta_{\mathbf{R}_{1},\mathbf{R}}\delta_{\alpha,\alpha_{1}}\delta_{s,s_{1}}\delta_{\eta,\eta_{1}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ |\psi\rangle=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{(\mathbf{R},\alpha\eta s)\in S_{fill}}\sum_{\eta_{2}s_{2}a}W_{a}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}:c_{\mathbf{k}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}^{\dagger}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}a\eta_{2}s_{2}}:\ |\psi\rangle

The spectrum of this matrix is again very simple, only
W_{1},\bm{W_{3}}
and zero eigenvalues, by the same proof as in the previous section
(Sec.
S2.3
)
. However, there is now a thermodynamic number of them,
N_{orb}:\nu_{f}:N_{M}
and the Weyl theorems do not give nontrivial results, as they only bound the new eigenvalues in between
\nu_{i}
and
\nu_{i-N_{orb}:\nu_{f}:N_{M}}
which can now be a large interval.
At large fillings (say
\nu_{f}=-3
), it could become convenient
Xie et al. 2022
to fill unbounded eigenvalues rather than just fill the particle continuum and hence a CDW or other translational breaking states could be competitive.

S3
Zero-hybridization Model with
{\hat{H}}_{J}

We next study the zero hybridization model with
{\hat{H}}_{J}
. The Hamiltonian is given below

\displaystyle{\hat{H}}_{c}+{\hat{H}}_{U}+{\hat{H}}_{W}+{\hat{H}}_{V}+{\hat{H}}_{J}

(S62)

where
{\hat{H}}_{J}
describes a ferromagnetic exchange coupling between flat U(4) moments (Eq.
S16
of
f
-electrons and
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}

c
-electrons (
a=3,4
)
Song and Bernevig 2022

\hat{H}_{J}=-J\sum_{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{q}}\sum_{\mu\nu}\sum_{\xi=\pm}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{R}}:\hat{\Sigma}_{\mu\nu}^{(f,\xi)}(\mathbf{R})::\hat{\Sigma}_{\mu\nu}^{(c\prime\prime,\xi)}(\mathbf{q}):

(S63)

S3.1
Coherent states of the
f
-moments.

Since
J\sim 16.38meV
is relatively small compared to
U
and
W
, we assume that
{\hat{H}}_{J}
term will not destroy the uniform charge distribution of the
f
-electrons in the plateau region of Fig.
S3

which includes
\nu=0,-1,-2
. To find the ground state at small but non-zero
J
, we do the following

•

Find
\nu_{f}
at given
\nu
at
J=0
limit
(which is given in Fig.
S3
)
.

•
